NeoGuard: clinician adjudication

Exactly 100 synthetic cases: NICE 40, AAP 30, WHO 30. No real patient data. All ratings are blank until a clinician supplies them. This is a research review packet.

Assess observations and candidate first; then open the proposed reference. Record scope errors, missing conditions and potential harm explicitly. Do not interpret source hashes as proof of clinical correctness.

Ratings CSV | Review handbook | Benchmark description

deterioration-1

NICE | deterioration | priority: standard

Clinical question

Review respiratory deterioration, hypoxia and abnormal vital signs over serial observations.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 12,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 35,
  "care_setting": "hospital",
  "respiratory_rate": 74,
  "spo2": 90,
  "crp": 4,
  "culture_status": "unknown",
  "red_flags": "not fully documented",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 3,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 14
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 6,
    "respiratory_rate": 60,
    "spo2": 94,
    "crp": 9
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. The infant shows red‑flag clinical indicators of possible early‑onset infection: tachypnoea (RR 74), hypoxia (SpO2 90%) and signs of respiratory distress.
  2. Because red‑flag indicators are present, NICE recommends initiating antibiotic therapy without awaiting investigation results.
  3. If antibiotics are started, continue newborn early warning system monitoring for at least 12 hours, recording respiratory rate, oxygen saturation and temperature serially.
  4. Escalate to intensive care if any of the following develop: need for mechanical ventilation, worsening hypoxia (SpO2 < 90% despite supplemental O₂), or signs of shock.
  5. Obtain blood cultures and a full sepsis work‑up (CBC, CRP, blood gas) promptly, as recommended for babies with red‑flag indicators.
  6. Discuss with parents the concern for early‑onset sepsis, the need for antibiotics, and the plan for close monitoring and possible escalation.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

The recorded respiratory rate increased and oxygen saturation fell over serial observations. Respiratory distress and hypoxia are clinical indicators of possible infection. Arrange immediate clinical assessment, review maternal and neonatal history, examine vital signs, and apply the guideline framework for multiple clinical indicators. Do not wait for test results when treatment is indicated; treatment decisions require the responsible clinician.

NICE-box2 | PDF page 14

Box 2: Clinical indicators of possible early-onset neonatal infection (observations and events in the baby), including 'red flags' Red flag clinical indicators: • apnoea (temporary stopping of breathing) • seizures • need for cardiopulmonary resuscitation • need for mechanical ventilation • signs of shock Other clinical indicators: • altered behaviour or responsiveness • altered muscle tone (for example, floppiness) • feeding difficulties (for example, feed refusal) • feed intolerance, including vomiting, excessive gastric aspirates and abdominal distension • abnormal heart rate (bradycardia or tachycardia) • signs of respiratory distress (including grunting, recession, tachypnoea) • hypoxia (for example, central cyanosis or reduced oxygen saturation level) • persistent pulmonary hypertension of newborns • jaundice within 24 hours of birth • signs of neonatal encephalopathy • temperature abnormality (lower than 36°C or higher than 38°C) unexplained by environmental factors • unexplained excessive bleeding, thrombocytopenia, or abnormal coagulation

Text SHA-256: 6e0637bd9a5b39440c57545a9d2f876470ef1f132548b52290e1e6fb843c4063

NICE-1.10.1 | PDF page 13

If there are any risk factors for early-onset neonatal infection (see box 1 on risk- factors for early-onset neonatal infection), or if there are clinical indicators of possible early-onset neonatal infection (see box 2 on clinical indicators of possible early-onset neonatal infection): • perform an immediate clinical assessment • review the maternal and neonatal history • carry out a physical examination of the baby, including an assessment of vital signs. [2021]

Text SHA-256: e8ab33fa671e7f79a318bbb9b9e1557955ca8d45cf9b0d48d66357b7da2c6c4f

NICE-1.10.3 | PDF page 15

Use the following framework, based on the risk factors in box 1 and the clinical indicators in box 2, to make antibiotic management decisions as directed: • In babies with any red flag, or with 2 or more 'non-red-flag' risk factors or clinical indicators: － follow the recommendations in the section on investigations before starting antibiotics and － start antibiotic treatment according to the recommendations in this guideline and － do not wait for the test results before starting antibiotics • in babies without red flags and only 1 risk factor or 1 clinical indicator, use clinical judgement to decide: － whether it is safe to withhold antibiotics and － whether the baby's vital signs and clinical condition need to be monitored. If monitoring is needed, continue for at least 12 hours using a newborn early warning system • for babies without risk factors or clinical indicators of possible infection, continue routine postnatal care as covered in NICE's guideline on postnatal care. [2021]

deterioration-2

NICE | deterioration | priority: standard

Clinical question

Review respiratory deterioration, hypoxia and abnormal vital signs over serial observations.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 18,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 36,
  "care_setting": "hospital",
  "respiratory_rate": 74,
  "spo2": 90,
  "crp": 5,
  "culture_status": "unknown",
  "red_flags": "not fully documented",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 3,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 15
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 6,
    "respiratory_rate": 60,
    "spo2": 94,
    "crp": 10
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. The infant shows tachypnoea (RR 74) and hypoxia (SpO2 90%) which are listed as clinical indicators of possible early‑onset neonatal infection.
  2. Apnoea, need for mechanical ventilation, and signs of shock are red‑flag indicators; while not yet present, the current respiratory deterioration warrants treating any red flag as a trigger for urgent antibiotic initiation.
  3. Because the baby has at least one red‑flag clinical indicator (hypoxia) and multiple non‑red‑flag indicators (tachypnoea, altered behaviour not documented), antibiotics should be started without awaiting culture results.
  4. If antibiotics are started, continue newborn early warning system monitoring for a minimum of 12 hours, recording respiratory rate, SpO2, heart rate and temperature at least hourly.
  5. Escalate to senior neonatal team or intensive care if any red‑flag develops (e.g., apnoea, need for CPAP/mechanical ventilation, worsening hypoxia <90% despite supplemental O₂).
  6. Obtain blood cultures, full blood count and CRP promptly; repeat CRP after 12 hours to assess trend, recognizing that an initial CRP of 5 mg/L is low but may rise with infection.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

The recorded respiratory rate increased and oxygen saturation fell over serial observations. Respiratory distress and hypoxia are clinical indicators of possible infection. Arrange immediate clinical assessment, review maternal and neonatal history, examine vital signs, and apply the guideline framework for multiple clinical indicators. Do not wait for test results when treatment is indicated; treatment decisions require the responsible clinician.

deterioration-3

NICE | deterioration | priority: standard

Clinical question

Review respiratory deterioration, hypoxia and abnormal vital signs over serial observations.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 24,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 37,
  "care_setting": "hospital",
  "respiratory_rate": 74,
  "spo2": 90,
  "crp": 6,
  "culture_status": "unknown",
  "red_flags": "not fully documented",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 3,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 16
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 6,
    "respiratory_rate": 60,
    "spo2": 94,
    "crp": 11
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. The infant shows red‑flag clinical indicators of possible early‑onset infection: tachypnoea (RR 74 bpm), hypoxia (SpO₂ 90 %) and signs of respiratory distress.
  2. Because a red‑flag is present, the guideline advises that antibiotics should be started without waiting for investigation results.
  3. If antibiotics are initiated, continue newborn early warning system monitoring for at least 12 hours, recording respiratory rate, oxygen saturation and heart rate serially.
  4. Escalate to senior neonatal team or intensive care if any of the following occur: need for mechanical ventilation, worsening hypoxia (SpO₂ < 90 % despite supplemental O₂), or development of shock.
  5. Obtain blood cultures, full blood count and CRP promptly, and consider a lumbar puncture if clinically feasible, as part of the recommended investigations before antibiotics.
  6. Ask parents or caregivers about any concerns regarding the baby’s behaviour, feeding or appearance, as recommended when assessing for early‑onset infection.

crp_alone-1

NICE | crp_alone | priority: standard

Clinical question

Explain whether a falling C-reactive protein alone excludes infection or permits stopping antibiotics.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 12,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 35,
  "care_setting": "hospital",
  "respiratory_rate": 48,
  "spo2": 97,
  "crp": 4,
  "culture_status": "unknown",
  "red_flags": "not fully documented",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 3,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 14
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 6,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 9
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. A decreasing C‑reactive protein (CRP) value by itself does not rule out neonatal infection and should not be used as the sole basis to stop antibiotics.
  2. Baseline CRP should be measured when antibiotics are started and serial CRP results interpreted together with clinical signs to gauge infection likelihood and treatment response.
  3. If antibiotics are continued beyond 36–48 hours, review the need daily, considering initial suspicion, clinical progress, and CRP trends before deciding to stop therapy.
  4. When reviewing after 36–48 hours, incorporate the baby's current condition, any change in CRP, and overall clinical trajectory to determine whether antibiotics can be safely discontinued.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

The C-reactive protein concentration fell in the supplied history. A falling result alone cannot determine whether to start, continue or stop antibiotics. Interpret serial C-reactive protein together with clinical condition and investigation results. Reassess the baby and review microbiology before a clinician makes treatment decisions.

NICE-1.15.3 | PDF page 25

Do not use C-reactive protein results alone to make decisions about starting, continuing or stopping antibiotics. [2026]

Text SHA-256: 8597b6e8db04ff76294d1f3c44162bc88431bd29eededadc66478a8abc288641

NICE-1.13.2 | PDF page 21

Measure baseline C-reactive protein concentration when starting antibiotic treatment in babies who may have neonatal infection. Use this together with any subsequent readings to assess the likelihood of infection and response to treatment. [2021]

Text SHA-256: bb97f03163df6f708c62d9706bb247652cf386f769fe2b5a5efa6b65e81ac778

NICE-1.15.4 | PDF page 25

Regularly reassess the clinical condition and results of investigations in babies receiving antibiotics. Consider whether to change the antibiotic regimen, taking account of: • the baby's clinical condition (for example, if there is no improvement) • the results of microbiological investigations • expert microbiological advice, including local surveillance data. [2012]

crp_alone-3

NICE | crp_alone | priority: standard

Clinical question

Explain whether a falling C-reactive protein alone excludes infection or permits stopping antibiotics.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 24,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 37,
  "care_setting": "hospital",
  "respiratory_rate": 48,
  "spo2": 97,
  "crp": 6,

  "red_flags": "not fully documented",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 3,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 16
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 6,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 11
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. A falling C‑reactive protein level by itself is insufficient to rule out infection or to justify stopping antibiotics in a neonate with possible early‑onset sepsis.
  2. Serial CRP measurements should be interpreted together with the infant's clinical progress and the initial suspicion of infection when reviewing antibiotic therapy.
  3. If antibiotics have been continued beyond 36–48 hours with negative cultures, conduct a formal review at least every 24 hours, incorporating CRP trends, clinical status, and initial risk assessment.
  4. Decision‑making should weigh the level of initial clinical suspicion, the baby's current condition, and CRP trajectory rather than relying on a single decreasing CRP value.

Do not use C-reactive protein results alone to make decisions about starting, continuing or stopping antibiotics. [2026]

incomplete_stop-1

NICE | incomplete_stop | priority: high

Clinical question

At 36 hours, culture is negative but symptoms persist. Explain the missing criteria for stopping antibiotics.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 36,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 35,
  "care_setting": "hospital",
  "respiratory_rate": 74,
  "spo2": 97,
  "crp": 4,
  "culture_status": "negative",
  "red_flags": "not fully documented",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 3,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 14
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 6,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 9
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. Stopping antibiotics at 48 h requires four criteria: negative blood culture, low initial suspicion of infection, a reassuring clinical condition with no infection indicators, and reassuring CRP levels/trends. At 36 h the culture is negative, but the remaining three criteria have not been documented, so antibiotics cannot be stopped yet.
  2. Hospital systems should deliver blood‑culture results within 36 hours of starting antibiotics for early‑onset sepsis to enable timely discontinuation and discharge.
  3. If intravenous antibiotics are continued beyond 36 hours despite a negative culture, the team must review the need for ongoing therapy at least every 24 hours, considering the initial suspicion level, the baby’s clinical progress, and CRP trends.
  4. For infants ≥35 weeks gestation who have received >36 hours of IV antibiotics because the initial suspicion was strong, consider switching to oral antibiotics once the clinical condition is reassuring, CRP is reassuring, and the baby tolerates oral feeds.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

A negative blood culture alone does not satisfy the stopping criteria at 36 hours. Initial suspicion must not have been strong, clinical condition must be reassuring with no indicators of possible infection, and C-reactive protein levels and trends must be reassuring. Persistent respiratory concerns mean these conditions are not all established. If antibiotics continue despite negative cultures, review their need at least every 24 hours with clinical progress and serial results.

NICE-1.21.3 | PDF page 30

For babies who are being treated with intravenous antibiotics because of risk factors for early-onset infection or clinical indicators of possible infection, stop antibiotics at 36 hours if: • the blood culture is negative and • the initial clinical suspicion of infection was not strong and • the baby's clinical condition is reassuring, with no clinical indicators of possible infection (see box 2 for clinical indicators of possible early-onset infection) and • the levels and trends of C-reactive protein concentration are reassuring. [2012, amended 2026]

Text SHA-256: 34fdaa8bc356906c84c3291386941435c2de0005cacbd2120164108a6f3eeabb

NICE-1.21.4 | PDF page 31

When continuing intravenous antibiotics for longer than 36 hours despite negative blood cultures, review the need for ongoing antibiotics at least once every 24 hours, taking account of: • the level of initial clinical suspicion of infection and • the baby's clinical progress and current condition and • the levels and trends of C-reactive protein concentration. [2012] 1.22 Switching to oral antibiotics

For babies who are being treated with intravenous antibiotics because of risk factors for early-onset infection or clinical indicators of possible infection, stop antibiotics at 36 hours if: • the blood culture is negative and • the initial clinical suspicion of infection was not strong and • the baby's clinical condition is reassuring, with no clinical indicators of possible infection (see box 2 for clinical indicators of possible early-onset infection) and • the levels and trends of C-reactive protein concentration are reassuring. single_risk-1

NICE | single_risk | priority: high

Clinical question

Review one known risk factor in a currently well baby without inventing absent findings.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 12,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 35,
  "care_setting": "hospital",
  "respiratory_rate": 48,
  "spo2": 97,
  "crp": 4,
  "culture_status": "unknown",
  "red_flags": "explicitly absent",
  "maternal_gbs": "positive"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 3,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 14
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 6,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 9
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. Maternal group B streptococcal colonisation is a recognized risk factor for early‑onset neonatal infection.
  2. Because a risk factor is present, an immediate clinical assessment and review of maternal and neonatal history should be performed.
  3. If antibiotics are not started immediately, the baby should be monitored for at least 12 hours using a newborn early warning system.
  4. In the presence of a risk factor, investigations (e.g., blood cultures, CRP) should be undertaken before antibiotics are given, but treatment should not be delayed awaiting results.
  5. Any emergence of red‑flag signs or a second risk factor requires prompt escalation to full antibiotic therapy per guideline recommendations.
  6. Explain to parents that maternal GBS colonisation places the baby at increased risk and that close monitoring and possible investigations are being undertaken.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

Review the maternal and neonatal history and perform a clinical examination with vital signs because a risk factor is present. For a baby with one risk factor and no red flags, clinical judgement determines whether antibiotics can safely be withheld and whether monitoring is required. If monitoring is needed, continue for at least 12 hours using a newborn early warning system. Unrecorded findings remain unknown.

NICE-1.10.1 | PDF page 13

If there are any risk factors for early-onset neonatal infection (see box 1 on risk- factors for early-onset neonatal infection), or if there are clinical indicators of possible early-onset neonatal infection (see box 2 on clinical indicators of possible early-onset neonatal infection): • perform an immediate clinical assessment • review the maternal and neonatal history • carry out a physical examination of the baby, including an assessment of vital signs. [2021]

NICE-1.10.3 | PDF page 15

Use the following framework, based on the risk factors in box 1 and the clinical indicators in box 2, to make antibiotic management decisions as directed: • In babies with any red flag, or with 2 or more 'non-red-flag' risk factors or clinical indicators: － follow the recommendations in the section on investigations before starting antibiotics and － start antibiotic treatment according to the recommendations in this guideline and － do not wait for the test results before starting antibiotics • in babies without red flags and only 1 risk factor or 1 clinical indicator, use clinical judgement to decide: － whether it is safe to withhold antibiotics and － whether the baby's vital signs and clinical condition need to be monitored. If monitoring is needed, continue for at least 12 hours using a newborn early warning system • for babies without risk factors or clinical indicators of possible infection, continue routine postnatal care as covered in NICE's guideline on postnatal care. [2021]

missing_results-1

NICE | missing_results | priority: high

Clinical question

Explain uncertainty when maternal history, culture results and a later clinical assessment are unavailable.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 12,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 35,
  "care_setting": "hospital",
  "respiratory_rate": 48,
  "spo2": 97,

  "maternal_gbs": "unknown"

[
  {
    "age_hours": 3,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 14
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 6,
    "respiratory_rate": 48,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 9
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. When maternal history, culture results, and later clinical assessment are unavailable, the presence of any known risk factors or clinical indicators for early‑onset neonatal infection cannot be confirmed, creating uncertainty about the infant’s infection risk.
  2. In the face of missing maternal and culture information, an immediate clinical assessment of the newborn should be performed to identify any observable signs of infection.
  3. A thorough physical examination, including vital‑sign monitoring (respiratory rate, SpO₂, temperature), should be undertaken repeatedly until maternal and microbiology data become available.
  4. If any clinical indicators of possible early‑onset infection emerge during monitoring (e.g., persistent tachypnoea, desaturation, rising CRP), escalation to full sepsis work‑up and empiric antibiotics is warranted despite absent maternal data.
  5. Interpretation of cerebrospinal fluid results, when obtained, must be integrated with the limited clinical picture and the unknown maternal history to avoid over‑ or under‑treatment.
  6. Explain to parents that the lack of maternal infection history and pending culture results introduces uncertainty, and that decisions will be guided by the newborn’s observed clinical status and ongoing monitoring.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

Maternal history, culture results and a later clinical examination are unavailable in this case. Missing information cannot be treated as normal findings or evidence against infection. Review the history, examine the baby and reassess vital signs and investigation results. Integrate serial laboratory measurements with clinical progress rather than making an automatic diagnosis or antibiotic decision.

NICE-1.10.1 | PDF page 13

If there are any risk factors for early-onset neonatal infection (see box 1 on risk- factors for early-onset neonatal infection), or if there are clinical indicators of possible early-onset neonatal infection (see box 2 on clinical indicators of possible early-onset neonatal infection): • perform an immediate clinical assessment • review the maternal and neonatal history • carry out a physical examination of the baby, including an assessment of vital signs. [2021]

aap-expanded-deterioration-01

Assess the rising respiratory rate and falling oxygen saturation during serial examinations; explain actions for an infant developing signs of illness. Gestation 37 weeks; age 6 hours; setting hospital birth.

Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)

Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

Serial observations show rising respiratory rate and falling oxygen saturation. Infants developing signs of illness require clinical evaluation; the AAP clinical-condition approach includes empirical treatment or further laboratory evaluation. Risk assessment must incorporate the infant examination and the local documented pathway. A reassuring birth examination does not establish that a currently ill infant is well.

AAP2018-35PLUS-P4-B5 | PDF page 4

A third strategy consists of the reliance on clinical signs of illness to identify infants with EOS. Under this approach, regardless of any estimation of neonatal or maternal risk factors for EOS, infants who appear ill at birth and those who develop signs of illness over the first 48 hours after birth are either treated empirically with antibiotic agents or further evaluated by laboratory screening. Among term and late- preterm infants, good clinical condition at birth is associated with a reduction in risk for EOS of approximately 60% to 70%. A multidisciplinary panel sponsored by the Eunice Kennedy Shriver National Institute of Child Health and Human Development advocated that infants be flagged for risk of EOS on the basis of the obstetric diagnosis of suspected intraamniotic infection but that those conducting newborn evaluation primarily rely on clinical observation alone for well-appearing term and late-preterm infants.

Text SHA-256: 73d5f7d43eacd1ad56023c8854d080b5d6c5c6412ec0aa9a9b535f5dc1b3b620

AAP2018-35PLUS-SP2 | PDF page 7

Infants born at ≥35 0/7 weeks’ gestation can be stratified by the level of risk for EOS. Acceptable approaches to risk stratification include the following: o categorical algorithms in which threshold values for intrapartum risk factors are used; o multivariate risk assessment based on both intrapartum risk factors and infant examinations. The Neonatal Early-Onset Sepsis Risk Calculator47 is an example of this approach; and o serial physical examination to detect the presence of clinical signs of illness after birth. This approach may begin with a categorical or multivariate assessment to identify newborn infants who are at risk and will be subjected to serial monitoring, or this may be applied to all newborn infants.

Text SHA-256: 950841afc18cad52bd3bac0723af7a8d9ca42078e93ddd5f4a9a1cc3f67d3ac3

Record adjudication in clinician_ratings.csv using this case ID. Case SHA-256: 81aeb8d2bf89b21f737cb8b76dde5679aa7cb6dcd0ee59d93be06c4a8adcbecd

Among term and late- preterm infants, good clinical condition at birth is associated with a reduction in risk for EOS of approximately 60% to 70%. A multidisciplinary panel sponsored by the Eunice Kennedy Shriver National Institute of Child Health and Human Development advocated that infants be flagged for risk of EOS on the basis of the obstetric diagnosis of suspected intraamniotic infection but that those conducting newborn evaluation primarily rely on clinical observation alone for well-appearing term and late-preterm infants.

Text SHA-256: 73d5f7d43eacd1ad56023c8854d080b5d6c5c6412ec0aa9a9b535f5dc1b3b620

AAP2018-35PLUS-SP2 | PDF page 7

Infants born at ≥35 0/7 weeks’ gestation can be stratified by the level of risk for EOS. Acceptable approaches to risk stratification include the following: o categorical algorithms in which threshold values for intrapartum risk factors are used; o multivariate risk assessment based on both intrapartum risk factors and infant examinations. The Neonatal Early-Onset Sepsis Risk Calculator47 is an example of this approach; and o serial physical examination to detect the presence of clinical signs of illness after birth. This approach may begin with a categorical or multivariate assessment to identify newborn infants who are at risk and will be subjected to serial monitoring, or this may be applied to all newborn infants.

aap-expanded-crp_alone-01

AAP | crp_alone | priority: standard

Clinical question

Can a single or falling CRP result exclude early-onset sepsis or alone determine whether antibiotics stop? Explain the limits of inflammatory markers. Gestation 35 weeks; age 6 hours; setting hospital birth.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 6,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 35,
  "care_setting": "hospital_birth",
  "respiratory_rate": 46,
  "spo2": null,
  "crp": 3,
  "culture_status": "unknown",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown",
  "red_flags": "not fully documented"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 2.0,
    "respiratory_rate": 46,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 18
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 4.0,
    "respiratory_rate": 46,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 9
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. A single CRP value obtained within the first hours of life is not sensitive or specific enough to rule out early‑onset sepsis (EOS) or to decide on stopping antibiotics.
  2. Consistently normal CRP (and procalcitonin) values over the first 48 hours are associated with the absence of EOS, but isolated or falling CRP alone cannot be used to exclude infection without culture data.
  3. Serial inflammatory markers (CRP, procalcitonin) should not be used as the sole assessment tool in well‑appearing term or late‑preterm infants; clinical status and risk factors remain primary drivers of monitoring.
  4. Continue empirical antibiotics until cultures are negative and the infant remains clinically stable, regardless of a single decreasing CRP, because laboratory tests alone cannot dictate discontinuation.
  5. Obtain blood (and, if indicated, CSF) cultures promptly; interpret CRP trends only in the context of culture results and the infant’s clinical trajectory.
  6. Escalate care (e.g., broaden antibiotics, respiratory support) if the infant shows worsening vital signs, rising inflammatory markers, or positive cultures, even when earlier CRP values were low or falling.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

Researchers in multiple studies address other markers of inflammation, including C-reactive protein (CRP), procalcitonin, interleukins (ILs) (soluble IL-2 receptor, IL-6, and IL-8), tumor necrosis factor α, and CD64. nice-expanded-incomplete_stop-01

NICE | incomplete_stop | priority: high

Clinical question

Blood culture is sterile after the recorded incubation, but respiratory illness persists. Explain antibiotic review and whether stopping conditions are established. Gestation 41 weeks; age 48 hours; setting hospital birth.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 48,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 41,
  "care_setting": "hospital_birth",
  "respiratory_rate": 76,
  "spo2": 97,
  "crp": 3,
  "culture_status": "negative",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown",
  "red_flags": "not fully documented",
  "culture_incubation_hours": 36,
  "antibiotic_duration_hours": 36,
  "clinical_condition": "persistent respiratory distress",
  "initial_suspicion": "not documented",
  "site_specific_infection": "not established"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 16.0,
    "respiratory_rate": 46,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 18
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 32.0,
    "respiratory_rate": 46,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 9
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. Because the blood culture remains sterile after 36 hours of incubation and the infant’s CRP has fallen from 18 mg/L at 16 h to 3 mg/L at 48 h, the guideline supports reviewing antibiotics and considering cessation.
  2. If the initial suspicion of infection was not strong and the baby’s clinical condition is reassuring (persistent respiratory distress but stable SpO₂ 97% and no worsening vitals), stopping antibiotics at 48 hours is appropriate.
  3. Continue to monitor respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, and CRP at least daily; if any clinical deterioration or rising CRP occurs, reassess the need for ongoing antibiotics.
  4. Escalate care (e.g., restart or extend antibiotics) if the baby develops new signs of infection, a rise in CRP, or if blood cultures become positive after extended incubation.
  5. Obtain repeat CRP and consider a chest radiograph if respiratory distress persists despite stable vitals, to rule out non‑infectious causes.
  6. Ensure the infant tolerates oral feeds before any switch to oral antibiotics, as recommended for term infants with reassuring condition.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

A negative blood culture alone does not satisfy the stopping criteria at the 36-hour review. Initial suspicion must not have been strong, clinical condition must be reassuring with no indicators of possible infection, and C-reactive protein levels and trends must be reassuring. Persistent respiratory concerns mean these conditions are not all established. If antibiotics continue despite negative cultures, review their need at least every 24 hours with clinical progress and serial results.

NICE-1.21.3 | PDF page 30

For babies who are being treated with intravenous antibiotics because of risk factors for early-onset infection or clinical indicators of possible infection, stop antibiotics at 36 hours if: • the blood culture is negative and • the initial clinical suspicion of infection was not strong and • the baby's clinical condition is reassuring, with no clinical indicators of possible infection (see box 2 for clinical indicators of possible early-onset infection) and • the levels and trends of C-reactive protein concentration are reassuring. [2012, amended 2026]

Text SHA-256: 34fdaa8bc356906c84c3291386941435c2de0005cacbd2120164108a6f3eeabb

NICE-1.21.4 | PDF page 31

When continuing intravenous antibiotics for longer than 36 hours despite negative blood cultures, review the need for ongoing antibiotics at least once every 24 hours, taking account of: • the level of initial clinical suspicion of infection and • the baby's clinical progress and current condition and • the levels and trends of C-reactive protein concentration. [2012] 1.22 Switching to oral antibiotics

Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

A negative blood culture alone does not satisfy the stopping criteria at the 36-hour review. Initial suspicion must not have been strong, clinical condition must be reassuring with no indicators of possible infection, and C-reactive protein levels and trends must be reassuring. Persistent respiratory concerns mean these conditions are not all established. If antibiotics continue despite negative cultures, review their need at least every 24 hours with clinical progress and serial results.

NICE-1.21.3 | PDF page 30

For babies who are being treated with intravenous antibiotics because of risk factors for early-onset infection or clinical indicators of possible infection, stop antibiotics at 36 hours if: • the blood culture is negative and • the initial clinical suspicion of infection was not strong and • the baby's clinical condition is reassuring, with no clinical indicators of possible infection (see box 2 for clinical indicators of possible early-onset infection) and • the levels and trends of C-reactive protein concentration are reassuring. [2012, amended 2026]

NICE-1.21.4 | PDF page 31

When continuing intravenous antibiotics for longer than 36 hours despite negative blood cultures, review the need for ongoing antibiotics at least once every 24 hours, taking account of: • the level of initial clinical suspicion of infection and • the baby's clinical progress and current condition and • the levels and trends of C-reactive protein concentration. [2012] 1.22 Switching to oral antibiotics

Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. For a 28‑week gestation infant at 12 h of age with possible early‑onset sepsis, obtain blood cultures, a complete blood count with differential, and C‑reactive protein as initial laboratory investigations; consider lumbar puncture if meningitis is suspected.
  2. Serial CRP measurements should be performed (e.g., at 4 h and 12 h) because a single early CRP may be normal despite infection.
  3. Pre‑term birth before 37 weeks and prolonged rupture of membranes (>18 h) are red‑flag risk factors that increase the pre‑test probability of early‑onset neonatal infection.
  4. If repeat CRP rises or the infant develops clinical deterioration (e.g., increasing respiratory rate, falling SpO₂), escalate to neonatal intensive care for broader antimicrobial coverage and possible invasive monitoring.
  5. Empiric antibiotics should be started promptly after cultures are drawn, guided by local protocols for pre‑term infants, while awaiting laboratory results.
  6. Explain to parents that laboratory tests for infection have limited early sensitivity; results may be normal initially, and treatment decisions rely on clinical risk factors and ongoing observation.

Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. Obtain a full blood culture before starting antibiotics; results may not be available for up to 36 hours, so early treatment decisions must rely on clinical assessment.
  2. Measure C‑reactive protein (CRP) and a complete blood count, recognizing that early CRP can be normal and trends over time are more informative than a single value.
  3. Identify red‑flag risk factors such as pre‑term birth (<37 weeks), prolonged rupture of membranes (>18 h pre‑term), maternal GBS colonisation, or chorioamnionitis, which increase the pre‑test probability of early‑onset sepsis.
  4. Continuously monitor vital signs (respiratory rate, SpO₂) and repeat CRP at 12‑hour intervals, as rising values may prompt escalation even if initial labs were equivocal.
  5. Escalate to full sepsis treatment if the infant shows clinical deterioration (e.g., increasing respiratory rate, falling SpO₂) or if serial CRP rises, acknowledging that laboratory tests alone cannot rule out infection.
  6. Start empirical antibiotics promptly after cultures are drawn, because laboratory confirmation is delayed and early therapy improves outcomes in high‑risk pre‑term neonates.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

aap-expanded-initial_investigation-01

AAP | initial_investigation | priority: standard

Clinical question

Explain appropriate initial diagnostic investigations for suspected early-onset sepsis and the limitations of laboratory tests. Gestation 36 weeks; age 6 hours; setting hospital birth.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 6,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 36,
  "care_setting": "hospital_birth",
  "respiratory_rate": 46,
  "spo2": null,
  "crp": 3,
  "culture_status": "unknown",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown",
  "red_flags": "not fully documented"
}

Earlier observations

[
  {
    "age_hours": 2.0,
    "respiratory_rate": 46,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 18
  },
  {
    "age_hours": 4.0,
    "respiratory_rate": 46,
    "spo2": 98,
    "crp": 9
  }
]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. Obtain a blood culture and basic laboratory panel (CBC, CRP) as initial diagnostic investigations for suspected early‑onset sepsis; blood culture is recommended when the estimated EOS risk is ≥1 per 1000 live births.
  2. Use maternal and infant clinical characteristics—including maternal GBS colonization, duration of rupture of membranes, and presence of chorioamnionitis—to stratify EOS risk and guide the decision to start empirical antibiotics.
  3. Implement serial physical examinations during the first 48‑72 hours to detect evolving signs of illness, recognizing that laboratory tests alone lack sufficient sensitivity and specificity.
  4. Escalate to full sepsis work‑up and empirical antibiotics if the infant becomes ill‑appearing, develops abnormal vital signs, or if repeat labs (e.g., rising CRP) suggest infection, because laboratory tests are not definitive.
  5. When risk assessment places the infant in a higher risk category (≥3 per 1000 live births), initiate empirical antibiotic therapy promptly while awaiting culture results.
  6. Explain to parents that early‑onset sepsis evaluation includes blood cultures and limited laboratory testing, but these tests may not detect infection early; close clinical observation is essential.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

Blood culture remains the diagnostic standard for EOS. Blood or CSF cultures establish the diagnosis; CBC and CRP alone cannot diagnose EOS. Surface cultures and gastric aspirates do not diagnose EOS, and urine culture is not indicated in EOS evaluations before 72 hours. Obtain and interpret diagnostic specimens within the responsible clinician assessment and local pathway; no available culture result is established here.

AAP2018-35PLUS-P5-B3 | PDF page 5

In the absence of validated, clinically available molecular diagnostics, blood culture remains the diagnostic standard for EOS. Newborn surface cultures and gastric aspirate analysis cannot be used to diagnose EOS, and urine culture is not indicated in sepsis evaluations performed at <72 hours of age. In modern blood culture systems, optimized enriched culture media with antimicrobial neutralization properties, continuous-read detection systems, and specialized pediatric culture bottles are used. Concerns have been raised about the incomplete detection of low-level bacteremia and the effect of intrapartum antibiotic administration. However, these systems are used to reliably detect bacteremia at a level of 1 to 10 colony-forming units per mL if a minimum blood volume of 1 mL is inoculated. Furthermore, researchers in several studies have reported no effect of intrapartum antibiotic therapy on time to positivity. Culture media containing antimicrobial neutralization elements efficiently neutralize β-lactam antibiotic agents and gentamicin. A median blood culture time to positivity of <24 hours is reported among term infants when using contemporary blood culture techniques. Despite the performance characteristics of modern blood cultures, a prolonged empirical antibiotic treatment of term newborn infants who are critically ill may occasionally be appropriate despite negative culture results.

Text SHA-256: 61d392efa321050e95546c97e0c54d0001d3b90e84384816bcb50e72d75f5185

AAP2018-35PLUS-SP4 | PDF page 7

The diagnosis of EOS is made by using blood or CSF cultures. EOS cannot be diagnosed by using laboratory tests, such as a complete blood cell count or CRP or by using surface cultures, gastric aspirate analysis, or urine culture.

Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. Initial diagnostic work‑up for suspected early‑onset sepsis should include blood culture and basic laboratory tests such as a complete blood count and C‑reactive protein, recognizing that these tests alone lack sufficient sensitivity and specificity to dictate management.
  2. Maternal and infant clinical characteristics—e.g., maternal GBS status, duration of rupture of membranes, and infant appearance—are used to stratify EOS risk and determine whether laboratory testing and empirical antibiotics are warranted.
  3. Blood culture remains the definitive test for confirming infection, but results are typically unavailable for 24–48 hours; a negative culture does not exclude early‑onset sepsis, so clinical judgment must continue while awaiting results.
  4. Because laboratory tests are limited, serial physical examinations and close monitoring of vital signs (respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, temperature) are essential during the first 48–72 hours to detect evolving signs of illness.
  5. When the estimated EOS risk is ≥1 per 1000 live births, obtain a blood culture and initiate enhanced clinical observation; if the risk is ≥3 per 1000, add empirical antibiotic therapy while awaiting culture results.
  6. Escalate to full sepsis management (including broad‑spectrum antibiotics and possible lumbar puncture) if the infant develops any concerning clinical signs such as persistent tachypnea, desaturation, or hemodynamic instability during serial exams.

Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. The infant has one categorical risk factor – maternal GBS colonization with adequate intrapartum antibiotics and no additional risk factors, placing him in category 4 of the AAP EOS algorithm.
  2. For category‑4 infants, the guideline recommends obtaining laboratory testing (e.g., CBC, CRP) while continuing observation.

  5. Document the risk assessment, the plan for observation, and the parental counseling regarding signs that should prompt immediate evaluation.

Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

For infants born at 35 weeks or later, acceptable AAP risk-assessment approaches include categorical risk algorithms, multivariate assessment incorporating intrapartum factors and infant examination, and serial physical examinations. Use a locally documented birth-center pathway with ongoing surveillance. A single known maternal risk does not establish EOS or justify an invented individual probability.

AAP2018-35PLUS-SP2 | PDF page 7

Infants born at ≥35 0/7 weeks’ gestation can be stratified by the level of risk for EOS. Acceptable approaches to risk stratification include the following: o categorical algorithms in which threshold values for intrapartum risk factors are used; o multivariate risk assessment based on both intrapartum risk factors and infant examinations. The Neonatal Early-Onset Sepsis Risk Calculator47 is an example of this approach; and o serial physical examination to detect the presence of clinical signs of illness after birth. This approach may begin with a categorical or multivariate assessment to identify newborn infants who are at risk and will be subjected to serial monitoring, or this may be applied to all newborn infants.

Birth centers should consider the development of locally tailored, documented guidelines for EOS risk assessment and clinical management. Ongoing surveillance once guidelines are implemented is recommended.

AAP2018-35PLUS-SP2 | PDF page 7

Infants born at ≥35 0/7 weeks’ gestation can be stratified by the level of risk for EOS. Acceptable approaches to risk stratification include the following: o categorical algorithms in which threshold values for intrapartum risk factors are used; o multivariate risk assessment based on both intrapartum risk factors and infant examinations. The Neonatal Early-Onset Sepsis Risk Calculator47 is an example of this approach; and o serial physical examination to detect the presence of clinical signs of illness after birth. This approach may begin with a categorical or multivariate assessment to identify newborn infants who are at risk and will be subjected to serial monitoring, or this may be applied to all newborn infants.

AAP2018-35PLUS-SP4 | PDF page 7

The diagnosis of EOS is made by using blood or CSF cultures. EOS cannot be diagnosed by using laboratory tests, such as a complete blood cell count or CRP or by using surface cultures, gastric aspirate analysis, or urine culture.

In young infants aged 0–59 days, the WHO 7-sign integrated management of childhood illness (IMCI) algorithm is recommended for the identification of infants with possible serious bacterial infection (PSBI) who require further evaluation or treatment. WHO2024-A.1 | PDF page 2

In young infants aged 0–59 days, the WHO 7-sign integrated management of childhood illness (IMCI) algorithm is recommended for the identification of infants with possible serious bacterial infection (PSBI) who require further evaluation or treatment. (Strong recommendation, moderate-certainty evidence)

Text SHA-256: 1c4cbd94cea526ed1907cca34a9d22c42f178b6980e7e0bcc5bc6303639a0258

WHO2024-A.1-DEFINITION | PDF page 2

Possible serious bacterial infection (PSBI) is defined by WHO as an illness with one or more of the following seven clinical signs: not feeding well or not able to feed at all, movement only when stimulated or no movement at all, high body temperature (≥ 38 °C), low body temperature (< 35.5 °C), severe chest indrawing, convulsions in infants aged 0–59 days or fast breathing (≥ 60 breaths per minute) in infants aged 0–6 days (see Table 1.1).

Clinical question

Does WHO 2024 endorse a hospital laboratory or clinical prediction algorithm to diagnose neonatal sepsis? Explain initial assessment without inventing a CBC or CRP threshold. Gestation 37 weeks; age 6 hours; setting hospital.

Earlier observations

[]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. WHO 2024 does not endorse a specific laboratory test or clinical prediction algorithm as the definitive diagnostic tool for early‑onset neonatal sepsis.
  2. The guideline notes that full blood count and C‑reactive protein (CRP) are increasingly available, but their use remains adjunctive rather than diagnostic alone.
  3. Initial assessment should combine clinical signs (e.g., temperature ≥ 38 °C, poor feeding, respiratory rate ≈ 48 bpm) with available bedside tests, while recognizing that no single threshold is mandated by WHO.
  4. Continuous monitoring of vital signs, feeding tolerance, and clinical status is recommended during the first 72 hours, with escalation if deterioration occurs.
  5. Escalate to higher‑level care or initiate empiric antibiotics if any red‑flag signs develop (e.g., worsening temperature, respiratory distress, or reduced movement).
  6. Explain to parents that diagnosis relies on a combination of clinical observation and limited laboratory data, and that treatment decisions will be revisited as the infant’s condition evolves.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

WHO made no recommendation for hospital-based clinical-sign diagnostic algorithms in section B.1. The stated reasons include feasibility limitations of laboratory-based algorithms and limited external validation. This is not a recommendation against all laboratory investigation. Clinical management of an unwell infant requires careful clinical judgement; no endorsed CRP or CBC cutoff or diagnostic score can be inferred from this source.

WHO2024-B.1 | PDF page 24

No recommendation Remarks ■The GDG reviewed 28 studies that recruited 138 575 infants aged 0–59 days in 20 countries – 17 LMICs: Bangladesh, Bolivia (Plurinational State of), Brazil, China, Ethiopia, Gambia, Ghana, India, Kenya, Pakistan, Papua New Guinea, the Philippines, South Africa, Thailand, Uganda, Viet Nam and Zimbabwe; and three high-income countries (HICs): Canada, Greece and Italy. ■All 28 studies compared assessment of clinical signs of sepsis with physician judgement of sepsis or mortality as the reference standard. The studies used a range of prediction models, weighted scores and checklists. ■The GDG decided not to make a recommendation on use of hospital-based algorithms for diagnosing sepsis as all studies used algorithms with laboratory tests that are not currently feasible in low-resource settings. ■The GDG recognized that some of these algorithms were routinely being used for risk stratification in HICs. However, they considered that evidence about these algorithms was limited to single studies with small sample sizes and did not have external validation. The GDG considered that further research is needed before recommendations on their use can be developed. ■The GDG proposed further research using harmonized and standardized assessment tools.

who-expanded-initial_investigation-03

WHO | initial_investigation | priority: high

Clinical question

Does WHO 2024 endorse a hospital laboratory or clinical prediction algorithm to diagnose neonatal sepsis? Explain initial assessment without inventing a CBC or CRP threshold. Gestation 40 weeks; age 24 hours; setting hospital.

Recorded observations

{
  "age_hours": 24,
  "gestational_age_weeks": 40,
  "care_setting": "hospital",
  "maternal_gbs": "unknown",
  "respiratory_rate": 48,
  "spo2": 97,
  "crp": null,
  "culture_status": "unknown",
  "temperature_c": 38.1,
  "feeding": "not feeding well",
  "movement": "spontaneous",
  "convulsions": false,
  "severe_chest_indrawing": false,
  "referral_possible": true,
  "red_flags": "not fully documented",
  "clinical_condition": "suspected sepsis"
}

Earlier observations

[]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. WHO does not endorse a specific hospital laboratory test or clinical prediction algorithm for diagnosing early‑onset neonatal sepsis; it highlights the current lack of simple, universally available tests in many district hospitals.
  2. When laboratory capacity exists, CRP and procalcitonin have shown good discriminatory ability for culture‑confirmed sepsis, but WHO provides no definitive threshold values for clinical decision‑making.
  3. Initial assessment should be based on a comprehensive clinical examination (temperature, respiratory rate, feeding tolerance, activity) and, if feasible, point‑of‑care biomarkers such as CRP or PCT to support the diagnosis.
  4. Continuous monitoring of vital signs (temperature, respiratory rate, SpO₂), feeding adequacy, and neurologic status is essential during the first 72 hours, with reassessment if any parameter deteriorates.
  5. If clinical signs worsen, red‑flag features emerge, or laboratory results become strongly suggestive of sepsis, clinicians should escalate care to a higher‑level facility or specialist team, as WHO notes ongoing work to integrate tests with decision‑support algorithms for timely escalation.
  6. WHO 2024 does not recommend a hospital laboratory or a specific clinical prediction algorithm for diagnosing neonatal sepsis; the guideline emphasizes clinical judgment supplemented by available biomarkers rather than a mandated scoring system.
Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

WHO made no recommendation for hospital-based clinical-sign diagnostic algorithms in section B.1. The stated reasons include feasibility limitations of laboratory-based algorithms and limited external validation. This is not a recommendation against all laboratory investigation. Clinical management of an unwell infant requires careful clinical judgement; no endorsed CRP or CBC cutoff or diagnostic score can be inferred from this source.

WHO2024-B.1 | PDF page 24

No recommendation Remarks ■The GDG reviewed 28 studies that recruited 138 575 infants aged 0–59 days in 20 countries – 17 LMICs: Bangladesh, Bolivia (Plurinational State of), Brazil, China, Ethiopia, Gambia, Ghana, India, Kenya, Pakistan, Papua New Guinea, the Philippines, South Africa, Thailand, Uganda, Viet Nam and Zimbabwe; and three high-income countries (HICs): Canada, Greece and Italy. ■All 28 studies compared assessment of clinical signs of sepsis with physician judgement of sepsis or mortality as the reference standard. The studies used a range of prediction models, weighted scores and checklists. ■The GDG decided not to make a recommendation on use of hospital-based algorithms for diagnosing sepsis as all studies used algorithms with laboratory tests that are not currently feasible in low-resource settings. ■The GDG recognized that some of these algorithms were routinely being used for risk stratification in HICs. However, they considered that evidence about these algorithms was limited to single studies with small sample sizes and did not have external validation. The GDG considered that further research is needed before recommendations on their use can be developed. ■The GDG proposed further research using harmonized and standardized assessment tools.

Proposed reference and gold passages - engineering authored, not clinician approved

WHO non-hospital identification uses the seven-sign IMCI algorithm. The cross-cutting remarks state that evidence was insufficient to recommend further stratification by gestation, birthweight or maternal risk factors. Maternal GBS history alone therefore does not yield a validated numerical WHO risk estimate. Do not infer absence of all infection or an automatic management order from the absence of recorded IMCI signs; use clinical judgement and the applicable local pathway.

WHO2024-A.1 | PDF page 2

In young infants aged 0–59 days, the WHO 7-sign integrated management of childhood illness (IMCI) algorithm is recommended for the identification of infants with possible serious bacterial infection (PSBI) who require further evaluation or treatment. (Strong recommendation, moderate-certainty evidence)

The cross-cutting remarks state that evidence was insufficient to recommend further stratification by gestation, birthweight or maternal risk factors. Maternal GBS history alone therefore does not yield a validated numerical WHO risk estimate. Do not infer absence of all infection or an automatic management order from the absence of recorded IMCI signs; use clinical judgement and the applicable local pathway.

WHO2024-A.1 | PDF page 2

In young infants aged 0–59 days, the WHO 7-sign integrated management of childhood illness (IMCI) algorithm is recommended for the identification of infants with possible serious bacterial infection (PSBI) who require further evaluation or treatment. (Strong recommendation, moderate-certainty evidence)

Earlier observations

[]
Candidate discussion points (assess before viewing proposed reference)
  1. In a primary health clinic, WHO recommends using a clinical sign‑based algorithm (e.g., the 7‑sign IMCI) to identify infants 0‑59 days with possible serious bacterial infection when laboratory data are unavailable.
  2. The algorithm has only fair sensitivity and specificity for detecting sepsis compared with physician judgement, so a negative assessment does not rule out infection.
  3. When feasible, WHO notes that biomarkers such as CRP or procalcitonin provide better discriminatory ability than clinical signs alone, but their diagnostic accuracy remains moderate (sensitivity ~0.62, specificity ~0.74).
  4. For a 60‑hour‑old term infant with unknown feeding, movement, temperature, and breathing status, the clinician should treat as possible serious bacterial infection and initiate empiric antibiotics per WHO EOS regimen while arranging referral if referral is possible.
  5. Serial clinical assessment (temperature, respiratory effort, feeding, activity) every 4‑6 hours is essential to detect deterioration, because the initial algorithm cannot capture all cases.
  6. If any danger sign (e.g., poor feeding, lethargy, fast breathing, chest indrawing, or temperature ≥ 38 °C or < 35.5 °C) emerges, immediate referral to a higher‑level facility is indicated.
